Natural-language rules for the teacher

What a teacher could state in words for each task, and where it comes from. The four datasets have four different provenances, and only two of them are independently validated — worth knowing before these are used as ground truth.

007bbfb7In the input, you should see...a 3x3 grid of black cells and cells of another color. The output grid size...is 9x9. To make the output, you have to...consider the 9x9 output grid as a 3x3 grid of 3x3 blocks. Each cell of the input (3x3 cells) thus corresponds to an output block (3x3 blocks). For each input cell, if the cell is black, then leave its corresponding block black as well ; else copy the whole input grid into the block.Adrien
025d127bThe input contains one or more colored outline shapes on a black background. Each shape has a lower-right L-shaped part made from its bottom horizontal row and the cell directly above the right end. The output grid size stays the same. Keep this lower-right L in place. Move every other colored cell in each shape one square to the right.Philipp
045e512cIn the input, you should see...a prototype (largest) shape and a couple of isolated colored shapes or cells around it on a unicolored background. The output grid size...is the same as the input grid size. To make the output, you have to...turn the surrounding colored shapes or cells into copies of the prototype (retaining their color), then, copy the offset from the original prototype (shortest path, diagonal is fine) to make more copies of the new shapes until the border is hit (partial shapes at the border are fine). The offset is copied at each new copy.Claas
09629e4fThe input and outbut are each an 11x11 grid with two grey rows and two grey columns partitioning the grid into a 3x3 arrangement of subgrids. Each of these subgrids is itself 3x3 cells. Each subgrid in the input contains the same number of uniquely colored cells, using the same palette, except for one. Use this subgrid as the template for the output, where each subgrid in the output is fully filled with the color of the corresponding cell in the input's odd subgrid. In all demonstrations here, each subgrid contains exactly five cells of different non-background colors—red, green, yellow, magenta, and cyan—except for the single odd subgrid, which omits cyan.Ryan
1f0c79e5In the input, you should see a 2*2 square placed anywhere in the grid. The output grid size stays the same. To make the output, you have to look at the red cells in the square in the input grid. There can be a maximum of 3 red cells. You have to recolor these cells in the other color present in the square (the square will only be of two colors maximum - red and some other color) and then extend the square in the direction that each red cell pointed to. Extending the square means extending each cell of the square diagonally till it hits an edge.Navya
2281f1f4In the input, you should see...a mostly black grid with grey cells scattered on the edges. The output grid size...stays the same as the input. To make the output, you have to...mark those cells red where a grey cell is at two orthogonal borders, in the same column (for example at the top) and in the same row (for example on the right).Claas
3345333eIn the input, you should see...a grid of black cells and other cells colored with up to two different colors. One of the colors composes some kind of object supposed to have a vertical symmetry axis, but the other color makes a rectangle that partially overlaps that object. The output grid size...stays the same. To make the output, you have to...remove entirely the colored rectangle and restore the vertical symmetry of the occluded object, by filling in with its color or black cells the cells where the rectangle was.Adrien
3428a4f5The input is a 5×13 grid. It contains two 5×6 red-and-black patterns separated by a horizontal yellow line. Create a 5×6 output by comparing the two patterns cell by cell. Make a cell green when exactly one of the two matching cells is red. Make it black when both matching cells are the same (either both black or both red).Philipp
3ac3eb23In the input, you should see...a grid with a unicolored background and a few single colored cells. The output grid size...is the same as the input grid size. To make the output, you have to...expand the colored shapes into a predefined pattern, which is 0-C-0, C-0-C, 0-C-0 and so forth until the border, where C is the relevant color and the first row is 0-C-0.Claas
447fd412The input contains a blue shape with red highlights, as well as at least one copy of these red highlights elsewhere in the grid without the (blue) rest of the shape. These highlights are placed as if the full shape had been copied, in some cases after having been magnified by an integer factor, and then the blue erased. Where these blue cells would have been erased, fill them back in using blue, resulting in a (possibly magnified) copy of the original shape. Do this for all instances of orphaned red highlights.Ryan
469497adIn the input, you should see a square and some colored rectangles stacked and attached to some of the edges of the grid. The output grid size stays the same. To make the output, you have to attach a diagonal red segment at each corner of the square that does not touch an edge of the grid. Each attachment must extend till it either hits the partial border made by the stack of rectangles or it hits an edge of the grid. You should end with the same original design as the input, but with these added red extensions/attachments to the square in the input.Navya
46f33fceIn the input, you should see...a grid with scattered colored cells. The output grid size...is the input grid size scaled up by 2 (e.g 10x10 becomes 20x20) To make the output, you have to...read the input as a grid of 2x2 blocks (a 10x10 input is a 5x5 arrangement of them). Each colored cell sits at an odd row and odd column, that is, in the bottom-right corner of its own 2x2 block, and no block holds more than one. Every block that holds a colored cell becomes a solid 4x4 box of that color at the same block position in the output; every other block is black.Claas
57aa92dbIn the input, you should see...a grid of black cells and of bi-colored objects. One of the colors is shared across all objects. The output grid size...stays the same. To make the output, you have to...identify the object with a single cell of the shared color and at least two cells of another color. Then, complete the other objects such that they become copies of that object but with their original second color, and by respecting their scale, which can be identified as the size of their square of cells of the shared color.Adrien
6aa20dc0The input contains one complete three-color shape that acts as a template. Elsewhere are incomplete versions showing only two of the template's colors. The output grid size stays the same. Complete each unfinished shape by adding cells of the third color. Scale and rotate the template as needed so that its two existing colored parts line up with the incomplete shape.Philipp
6e19193cIn the input, you should see...a unicolored background containing a few same-colored, three-cell L-shaped objects. Each object occupies three of the four cells of a 2×2 square. The output grid size...is the same as the input grid size. To make the output, you have to...for each L-shaped object, identify the missing fourth cell of its 2×2 square. The diagonal direction from the L's elbow cell toward this missing cell determines the direction in which the object points. Leave the missing cell empty, but continue one step farther in the same diagonal direction and start a line there. Extend this diagonal line, in the same color as the object, until it reaches the edge of the grid. Repeat this independently for every L-shaped object.Claas
73251a56The input grid is 21x21 cells and partitioned into various wedge-like sections or patterns by lines of various slopes originating from the top left corner. Each grid is drawn the same way, using randomized colors for each section. Imagine beginning with a blank canvas. First, a 45-degree diagonal line is drawn, bisecting the grid (and forming the grid's axis of symmetry); then, diagonal lines of slopes 2 and 1/2 are drawn using a different color and the area between them filled with that color (without overwriting any previously colored cells); then, diagonal lines of slopes 3 and 1/3 are drawn with a different color and the uncolored area between them filled with that color; and so on, until the entire grid has been colored this way, leaving no blank areas left. Finally, several "holes" are cut in the grid using black, interrupting the pattern and making it incomplete. The output grid shows the completed grid with the black "holes" filled in according to the original pattern.Ryan
7837ac64In the input, you will see a series of colored rows intersecting with colored columns in regular intervals to form squares. These rows and columns will be in the same color. The output grid size will be 3*3. To make the output, you have to find all the different colored corners of each square in the input to indicate/highlight squares in a 3*3 grid. Each of these 4 corners will correspond to that color cell in a 3*3 grid, so color the output grid cells according to the placement of each set of 4 corners and their colors.Navya
8403a5d5In the input, you should see...a single colored cell. The output grid size...is the same as the input grid size. To make the output, you have to...extend the colored cell in its current direction (whichever direction is free, if it is at the bottom extend to the top, if at the top extend to the bottom) until it reaches the opposite border. Place a gray cell immediately to the right of the endpoint. From that gray cell place a colored cell to the right of it and start a new colored column extending in the opposite vertical direction until it reaches the other border. Again, place a gray cell immediately to the right of the endpoint. Repeat this alternating procedure, moving one column to the right each time, until the right border of the grid is reached.Claas
8f2ea7aaIn the input, you should see...a 9x9 grid of black cells and some colored cells. The output grid size...stays the same. To make the output, you have to...consider the 9x9 input and output grids of cells as 3x3 grids of 3x3 blocks. The colored cells of the input grid should be contained in one of these blocks. For that specific block, consider that each cell corresponds to a block in the output grid. For a black cell, leave the corresponding output block black as well. For a colored cell, copy that specific block into the corresponding output block.Adrien
91413438The input is a 3×3 grid containing black cells and cells of one other color. Count the number of black cells and the number of colored cells. Make a square output whose height and width are three times the number of black cells. Treat the output as a grid of 3×3 spaces. Starting at the top left and moving left to right, row block by row block, place one copy of the original 3×3 pattern for each colored cell in the input. Leave all remaining space black.Philipp
9aec4887In the input, you should see...An irregular cyan shape and a multi-colored rectangle with missing corners. The output grid size...is the shape of the multi-colored rectangle To make the output, you have to...Insert the cyan shape into the multi-colored rectangle. For each part of the cyan shape that touches exactly one of the original colored lines that make up the rectangle, the cells are recolored from cyan to the touching color. If multiple of the original colored cells of the rectangle touch a cyan cell, or the cyan cell does not originally touch parts of the colored rectangle (only after initial recoloring), they stay cyan. The output is just the rectangle cut out with the cyan inside it.Claas
9d9215dbThe input grid is 19x19 cells and contains a "seed" of the pattern completed in the output grid. First, make the grid rotationally symmetric by copying the colored pattern three times, once in each corner empty corner, rotated appropriately. Then, identify any cells not on the diagonals of the grid. These cells "branch off" the diagonals and should be symmetric across the diagonals. Extend each of these "branching" cells as a dotted line parallel to the edge of the grid until the dotted lines meet and form a complete square (where each side of the square is dotted) to obtain the output grid. This resulting grid should still be rotationally symmetric.Ryan
a78176bbIn the input you will see a diagonal line of one non-grey color that connects two edges and grey staircases(s) attached to either side of the diagonal line. The output grid size stays the same. To make the output, keep the diagonal line and make a scaled up or down version of the same line at the point each grey staircase ends. If there is a grey staircase at either side of the diagonal, then there will be a scaled version of the diagonal at either side. Then remove the grey staircases shapes.Navya
ac0a08a4In the input, you should see...a grid with single colored cells. The output grid size...is the size of the input grid multiplied with the number of colored cells (e.g a 3x3 input grid with 4 colored cells has a 12x12 output grid). To make the output, you have to...scale up every colored cell according to the overall count of colored cells. If there are two colored cells, each cell is scaled up to 2x2 box (the relative position stays the same). If there are three colored cells, each turns into a 3x3 box and so forth.Claas
b527c5c6This is the same task as ARC-AGI-2 df9fd884 task that I did hereafter. I am just re-shaping the phrasing to correspond to LARC-style. In the input, you should see... a grid with mostly orange cells that act as background, two yellow U-shapes, one touching the upper side of the grid, the other touching the upper side of it (and being turned downward), and another shape acting as an object that can be moved, of a different color. That shape spans the same column(s) as the open part of the lower U-shape. The output grid size...stays the same. To make the output, you have to...first copy the input grid. Then, move the object such that it touches the lower side of the grid and spans the same column(s) as the open part of the upper U-shape. The initial position of the object becomes orange.Adrien
b548a754The input contains a two-color rectangle (one edge color and a different fill color) and a single light-blue marker outside it. The output grid size stays the same. Stretch the rectangle only in the direction of the light-blue marker, stopping when its outer edge reaches the marker (inclusive so the rectangle’s edge is now at the height or width where the marker has been initially). Continue the rectangle's border color around the new outside edge and its inner color through the enlarged interior.Philipp
caa06a1fIn the input, you should see...a multi-colored pattern and a unicolored background. The output grid size...is the same as the input grid size To make the output, you have to...shift the pattern by one to the left (this is pure recoloration, no actual shift of columns and no expension or shrinkage of the pattern size), then expand it to cover the rest of the background that is not colored using the pattern yet.Claas
cbded52dThe input is 8x8 cells containing 9 2x2 blue squares in a 3x3 arrangement, separated by rows and columns of black cells. Some blue squares have an extra feature in the form of one of its cells being recolored in a different color. If two of a given blue square's immediate (perpendicular, not diagonal) neighbors on opposite sides have the same feature—meaning the same non-blue color in the same relative position—in the input grid, it should then adopt that feature in the output grid. Squares that already have a feature in the input grid can still adopt new ones, and a square can adopt multiple at the same time.Ryan
cdecee7fIn the input, you will see several colored cells. There cannot be more than 9 colored cells in the input and no two colored cells will be in the same column. The output grid size will be 3*3. To make the output, populate the output grid with the colored cells in the order they appear column-wise in the input. The first row of the output will be populated left-right in that order, the next row will be populated right to left in that order and the final row will be populated left-right again in that order. There may be less than 9 colored cells in the input, so stop coloring the output once all colored cells from the input are reflected in the grid.Navya
d07ae81cIn the input, you should see...a regular background pattern with a few outlier cells which are of a different color The output grid size...is the same as the input grid size. To make the output, you have to...extend the colored outlier cells in all four diagonal directions. Whenever the extension crosses into another background color, it triggers a color change to the other outlier color. Extensions do not overwrite each otherClaas
d13f3404In the input, you should see...a 3x3 grid of black or colored cells. The output grid size...is 6x6. To make the output, you have to...first copy the input grid into the upper left 3x3 block of the output grid. Then, extend each color in the down-right diagonal direction up to the grid boundary. MARC2, task 00576224:The input is a 2×2 grid containing four distinct colors, one per cell.Create a 6×6 output grid by tiling the 2×2 input in a checkerboard pattern of tiles. Repeat the original 2×2 tile 3 times across to fill each row pair. For even row-pairs (rows 0-1 and 4-5), use the original tile as-is. For odd row-pairs (rows 2-3), use the tile with its two columns swapped (i.e., the left and right columns exchange positions). This produces an alternating tile pattern vertically.The output grid is 6 rows by 6 columns, exactly 3× the input width and 3× the input height. It is fully filled with no background color — every cell is determined by the tiling rule.Adrien
d22278a0The input contains colored markers (single colored cells) in some of the corners of an otherwise black grid. The output grid size stays the same. From each colored corner, draw larger and larger L-shaped square outlines of the same color, with one black row or column between consecutive outlines. Continue the patterns inward, but stop or shorten them where different colors would overlap or touch along an edge. Different colors may touch diagonally. MARC2, task 00576224:The input is a 2×2 grid containing four distinct colors, one per cell.Create a 6×6 output grid by tiling the 2×2 input in a checkerboard pattern of tiles. Repeat the original 2×2 tile 3 times across to fill each row pair. For even row-pairs (rows 0-1 and 4-5), use the original tile as-is. For odd row-pairs (rows 2-3), use the tile with its two columns swapped (i.e., the left and right columns exchange positions). This produces an alternating tile pattern vertically.The output grid is 6 rows by 6 columns, exactly 3× the input width and 3× the input height. It is fully filled with no background color — every cell is determined by the tiling rule.Philipp
d406998bIn the input, you should see...a scattered grey pattern on a unicolored background The output grid size...the same size as the input grid To make the output, you have to...enumerate all grey cells using their column index (e.g from left to right, top to bottom order does not matter). Every other grey cell is (e.g the second, third, fifth...) recolored to green.Claas
d6ad076fThe input grid contains two rectangles of different colors arranged parallel to each other. First measure the lengths of the rectangles in whichever direction the two are parallel. Then, bridge the gap between the two using cyan. The width of this bridge should be 2 cells fewer than the shorter of the two measured lengths, and should be aligned such that the bridge's center is aligned with the center of the shorter rectangle.Ryan
f1cefba8In the input, you will see one larger rectangle of one color and a smaller rectangle of a different color placed at its center. Some rows/columns of the smaller rectangle will be longer than others by one cell, essentially looking like extensions/attachments. The output grid size stays the same. To make the output, first copy the exact same design as the input. Wherever there are extensions to the smaller rectangle, extend those till they hit an edge of the grid. Then, erase the parts of the extended rows/columns that are on top of the bigger rectangle, such that the smaller rectangle becomes divided into smaller parts and the extensions protrude from different sides of the big rectangle and connect it to edges of the grid.Navya
fafffa47In the input, you should see...a grid that is divided into an upper and a lower part of equal size, which are populated by different colored cells. The output grid size...has the same width, but only half the height. To make the output, you have to...treat the upper and lower halves as separate grids. For each cell in the output grid, check whether the same position is filled or empty in both reference grids. If it is empty in both, color the cell with light red (2). Otherwise, leave it empty.Claas

To make the output, you have to...look at the color that form the design in the input.Find any empty areas of blocks that are enclosed by the lines that form the main design, fill them yellow.You should end with the same original design from the input, but with the empty areas filled yellow.
017c7c7bIn the input, you should see...a grid that is 3x6 with black and blue. The output grid size...add 3 rows to the bottom. To make the output, you have to...keep the original pattern, change the blue to red, and then continue the pattern in the new 3 rows.

You will notice that the left and right parts are 3x3. For each square that is colored on both the left and right parts, color the output grid with red on the new 3x3.

At the end you'll have the light blue cube in the same exact spot as in the input grid and the the other shape will be the same but moved directly towards and touching the blue cube.

You should result in nine squares the color of the center and eight the color of the original plus sign.

Start from the first colored pixel and create a line that goes to the opposite side using that color. Then go to the second colored pixel and do the same. Continue the pattern of stripes to the RIGHT for a long grid or go DOWN for a tall grid all the way to the end.

Do nothing with light blue or magenta squares
0d3d703eIn the input, you should see...a 3 by 3 grid with three different colored column. The output grid size...is also 3 by 3. To make the output, you have to...change the column color to a specific other color. If the original color is dark red, change it to pink. If it is green, change it to yellow. If dark blue, change to grey. If light blue, change to dark red. The opposite is also true (for example if it is light blue, change to dark red).
0dfd9992In the input, you should see...a mostly filled in grid with a few black holes. The output grid size...is the same as the input grid. To make the output, you have to...start by copying the input grid exactly. Fill in all of the black boxes to complete the pattern until there are no more black boxes. To do this notice the pattern of the colored boxes. For example the top row might repeat every 4th row (or 5 rows or whatever the number of rows is before it repeats). Once you've figured out the pattern just fill in the black boxes with the correct colored boxes. In the end you should see repeating patterns of colored boxes and there should be no black boxes in the grid.

10fcaaa3In the input, you should see... 1 or 2 squares filled in with the same color on a black background. The output grid size... is double the height and width of the input grid. To make the output, you have to... resize the output grid to be double the width and height of the input grid. Then, copy and paste each colored square in the input grid 4 times -- once in each "quadrant" (if, in the input, the square is in the uppermost and leftmost corner, then it should be in the uppermost and leftmost corner in the output, as well as in the uppermost and leftmost corner of each "quadrant"). Then, for every single colored square, do the following: put a light blue square on each adjacent corner, forming a cross with 4 light-blue squares and the colored square in the middle.

070dd51eThe input is a grid containing empty cells and colored cells, two per color present in the grid. The cells sharing a color are either on the same row or in the same column in the grid, with a variable number of only empty cells between them. To produce the output grid, first copy the input grid. Then, for each pair of cells sharing the same color and aligned in the same row, fill in the cells between them with that same color, in order to connect them with a line. Finally, proceed in the same way for the pairs aligned in the same column, overwriting any color that may already be present.Adrien

00576224The input is a 2×2 grid containing four distinct colors, one per cell. Create a 6×6 output grid by tiling the 2×2 input in a checkerboard pattern of tiles. For odd row-pairs (rows 2-3), use the tile with its two columns swapped (i.e., the left and right columns exchange positions). This produces an alternating tile pattern vertically. The output grid is 6 rows by 6 columns, exactly 3× the input width and 3× the input height. It is fully filled with no background color — every cell is determined by the tiling rule.

0f63c0b9The input is a 15x15 grid with a black background containing several single colored pixels (dots) scattered at various positions. Each dot is a different color. Ignore column positions. 2. Sort the dots by row index from top to bottom. 3. Partition the 15 rows into consecutive bands by computing midpoints between adjacent dots' row indices. The boundary between two adjacent dots at rows r1 and r2 is floor((r1 + r2) / 2) + 1, meaning the first band ends at row floor((r1+r2)/2) and the next band starts at floor((r1+r2)/2)+1. Both input and output grids are 15x15. The background color is black (.). The input contains exactly N single-colored pixels (N varies per puzzle instance) placed at distinct positions. 137f0df0The input is a 10×10 grid with black (.) background containing several 2×2 grey (X) blocks arranged in a semi-regular grid pattern. The blocks are separated by gap rows and gap columns of black cells. Some gaps appear between adjacent blocks (interior gaps), while other gaps appear at the edges of the grid beyond the outermost blocks (edge gaps). The blocks always come in 2×2 squares, and the gaps between them are either 1 or 2 cells wide. The arrangement varies per instance — blocks may be shifted, and edge gaps may appear on different sides. 1. Similarly identify interior gap columns: columns that lie between two groups of block columns. 3. 13f06aa5The input grid has a uniform background color filling most cells. Embedded in the grid are one or more small cross-shaped or T-shaped figures composed of a secondary color. 140c817eThe input grid has a uniform background color with several blue (B) cells scattered at various positions. These blue cells serve as seed points for a cross-based transformation. Red and green never conflict since green is placed on diagonals while red sits at cross intersections. All cells not affected by any of these rules remain the background color. The grid dimensions are the same in input and output. The background is a single uniform color (whichever non-blue color fills the input). The coordinate system is row-column with (0,0) at top-left. This produces a staircase of nested L-shapes using the band colors, with the diagonal shifted one level down. This produces a regular grid pattern where full rows and columns of color C appear at intervals of S, with NxN black cells filling each compartment between the colored lines. The output grid is always 15x15. The background is black. The colored lines form a cross-hatch or lattice pattern whose spacing is determined by the input grid size N, with colored lines at every (N+1)th position starting at index N.
6a11f6daThe input is a tall grid consisting of three equally-sized square layers stacked vertically. Each layer is 5 rows by 5 columns. The top layer contains cells that are either blue (B) or black (.). The middle layer contains cells that are either azure (A) or black (.). The bottom layer contains cells that are either magenta (M) or black (.). Each layer acts as a binary mask for its respective color, where the colored cells are 'present' and black cells are 'absent.' Divide the input into three equal layers from top to bottom: the B-layer (blue), the A-layer (azure), and the M-layer (magenta). Each layer has the same dimensions. Create an output grid of the same dimensions as one layer. For each cell position, apply a priority rule to determine the output color: if the M-layer has a colored cell at that position, the output is M (magenta); otherwise, if the B-layer has a colored cell at that position, the output is B (blue); otherwise, if the A-layer has a colored cell at that position, the output is A (azure); otherwise, the output is black (.). In short, the priority order is M > B > A > black. The input grid is 15 rows by 5 columns, composed of three stacked 5×5 layers. The output grid is 5 rows by 5 columns. The background color is black (.). The three colors used are blue (B), azure (A), and magenta (M), each exclusive to its respective layer in the input but potentially co-occurring at the same position in the output resolution.
6d1d5c90The input grid has N rows and 7 columns. The first column (column 0) is filled entirely with the color magenta (M), except for exactly one cell which is red (R). Equivalently, the new row order starts at row (N - r) mod N and wraps around. In other words, the output row order is: row (N-r) mod N, row (N-r+1) mod N, ..., row (N-r-1) mod N. The grid boundaries act as the termination condition for the path — once the path would move outside the grid, it stops. The original colored cells (G, M, A) are preserved in place; only the path cells are added as green.
e633a9e5A 3x3 grid of colored cells. Each cell contains one of several possible colors. The grid has three rows and three columns, with no special background color or distinguished elements — every cell is equally significant. Expand the 3x3 input grid into a 5x5 output grid by duplicating the border rows and columns while keeping the center row and column single. Specifically: (1) Duplicate each column: the first column appears twice, the middle column appears once, and the last column appears twice, producing 5 columns per row. (2) Duplicate each row: the first row appears twice, the middle row appears once, and the last row appears twice, producing 5 rows total. In index terms, output row i maps to input row [0,0,1,2,2][i], and output column j maps to input column [0,0,1,2,2][j]. Input is always a 3x3 grid. Output is always a 5x5 grid. There is no background color; all cells carry meaningful color values. The transformation preserves all original colors and spatial relationships, only scaling the grid by stretching the border rows and columns.
e681b708The input grid has a framework of blue (B) lines running horizontally and vertically, dividing the space into rectangular cells. At certain intersections and endpoints of these grid lines, there are colored corner markers — non-blue, non-black symbols such as R (red), G (green), A (azure), Y (yellow), M (magenta), etc. These colored markers always sit on the grid lines themselves, typically at corners where lines meet or at the ends of lines along the grid border. Inside the rectangular cells formed by the grid lines, there are scattered blue (B) pixels on an otherwise black (.) background. These scattered B's are distinct from the structural B's that form the grid lines. 1. Identify all grid lines: continuous horizontal and vertical runs of B's (and colored markers) that span the width or height of a cell region. Colored markers embedded in these lines are part of the framework. 2. Identify the rectangular cells formed by adjacent grid lines. 3. For each cell, find the colored (non-B) corner markers at the intersections or endpoints of the grid lines that bound that cell. Collect all such corner colors. 4. Determine the majority color among those corners (the color that appears most frequently). 5. Leave all grid-line B's, corner markers, and black background pixels unchanged. Grids vary in size (roughly 20-26 rows/columns). The background is black (.). Blue (B) lines form a rectangular partition of the grid into cells of varying sizes. Colored markers at grid-line intersections and endpoints use colors from the ARC palette (R, G, A, Y, M, etc.). The grid lines, corner markers, and black cells are invariant; only the scattered non-grid-line B's within cells change color.
e69241bdThe input grid contains three types of cells: wall cells marked X, empty cells marked . (black), and a small number of colored marker cells (each a distinct non-X, non-. color). For each colored marker cell in the grid, perform an orthogonal flood fill starting from that marker's position. The fill spreads to all adjacent cells (up, down, left, right) that are empty (. cells), continuing recursively through connected empty cells. X cells act as impassable walls that block the spread. Every empty cell reached by the flood fill is recolored to match the marker's color. The marker cell itself retains its color. X cells and other marker cells are never overwritten. If two markers' flood regions would never overlap (because X walls separate them), each simply fills its own connected region independently. The grid dimensions match the input exactly (no resizing). The grid uses a standard row-column coordinate system. The background is . (black), walls are X, and colored markers are any other character. Output preserves all X cells and marker cells in their original positions; only . cells change, becoming the color of whichever marker can reach them via orthogonal connectivity through . cells.
e74e1818The input grid contains several colored shapes arranged as vertically stacked blocks against a black background. Each distinct non-black color occupies a contiguous band of consecutive rows, forming a group. These color groups are stacked on top of each other with no vertical gaps between them (though they may not span the full width of the grid). Within each color group, the rows may differ in width and pixel pattern, giving each group an asymmetric top-to-bottom profile. For each color group (a set of consecutive rows sharing the same non-black color), reverse the vertical order of its rows in place. That is, the first row of the group swaps with the last, the second with the second-to-last, and so on. Each group stays in the same vertical position within the grid — only the internal ordering of its rows is flipped. The horizontal positions of all pixels remain unchanged. Repeat this for every color group independently. The grid has a black (.) background. Its dimensions vary but include a border of black cells around the colored content. The colored regions collectively tile a vertical band of rows in the interior of the grid without overlap or gaps between adjacent color groups. Each color appears in exactly one contiguous vertical band of rows.

test2_t12The input contains a straight full-length line of 9s and, touching it, a hollow shape open on one side — a C, a V, a diagonal cup, or a ring with one gap. Other coloured cells lie scattered around. In the output, the shape has slid in the direction of its opening, one cell at a time, pushing along every coloured cell it ran into (and everything those pushed cells ran into), until the shape or something it was pushing would have left the grid or hit the line of 9s. Cells it never reached, and the line of 9s, are unchanged.Claas

taskrule
test2_t1Corner-components to center-components generator for Test2/t1. The rule matches Test2/t1.json: - up to four components, one per corner window - each corner component is translated to the corresponding center window
test2_t10180° partner fill (center-symmetric sparse overlay. Rule: - For each cell (r, c): output[r, c] = input[r, c] if nonzero, else input[H - 1 - r][W - 1 - c] (180° partner). Training-aligned input constraint (not required by the map algebra, but matches t10.json): - On every 180° orbit, at most one cell is nonzero in the input. Equivalently, no nonzero cell sits on top of an orbit that already has a nonzero on its partner — so partner cells stay empty and each orbit has a single “source” color that fills both endpoints in the output (output is constant on each orbit).
test2_t11Diagonal rays with wall bounces. Rule: - Input has exactly one non-zero start cell (source color). - From start, shoot along all 4 diagonal directions. - For each direction: 1) March to the first wall (boundary cell), coloring visited cells with source color. 2) Bounce off that wall and move the same number of steps again, with reflective boundary behavior (can bounce multiple times while consuming remaining steps). 3) Mark the final cell blue (1). - Finally, color each final cell's 4 diagonal neighbors green (3), potentially overwriting existing colors.
test2_t13Concentric rectangular layers + green marker + score stack. Rule (matches Test2/t13.json demos): - Input is a 0-padded grid with a solid axis-aligned rectangle of non-zero cells. - Exactly one cell is green (3), marking a position inside the rectangle. - Concentric layers are onion rings with configurable per-ring thickness; scoring follows the same logical-layer peel rule as verifier.transform_t13 (deeper layer → higher score). - Output equals input except: - the green cell becomes the true color of its layer; - in the rightmost column, the bottom score cells are set to brown/red (9), overwriting.
test2_t16Edge fragments assemble to a target grey shape. Orthogonally-connected task-color islands are fused transitively whenever they king-touch, so diagonal contact defines a single prototype (no special-case ridge split). Interior grey is matched on 4-connectivity only.
test2_t17Pattern fall. The prototype is always fully visible somewhere on the grid (an unclipped copy), with at least one placement strictly inside the outer border (not on row/column 0 or the last row/column). Prefer strictly interior 4‑connected foreground blobs of size 2–10 (unique normalised shape, or most frequent among ties). If that does not determine the prototype (merged blobs, ambiguous fragments), fall back to the largest n≤10 connected shape that occurs as an induced subgraph of the foreground (orthogonal connectivity first, then king) and admits an exact disjoint cover of the foreground.
test2_t18Horizontal periodic pattern wrapping between halves. Rule: a horizontally periodic motif occupies one half of the grid, with its axis row reaching one of the side edges (left or right) and NOT touching the opposite edge. The motif then "wraps" into the other half on the SAME side as the wrap edge, just shifted vertically into the other half. From there it continues across the other half all the way to the opposite grid edge. The periodic structure is preserved across the wrap. Equivalently, parameterize the wave by an integer position x that increases along its path from the input-half interior, out to the wrap edge, then jumps across to the same edge in the other half and proceeds to the opposite edge: - Input half, wrap=right: col c -> x = c. (col 0 interior, col W-1 wrap edge) - Other half, wrap=right: col c -> x = 2*W - 1 - c. (col W-1 wrap edge, col 0 opposite) - Input half, wrap=left : col c -> x = W - 1 - c. (col W-1 interior, col 0 wrap edge) - Other half, wrap=left : col c -> x = W + c. (col 0 wrap edge, col W-1 opposite) Cells are drawn at (axis_row + dr, c) iff (dr, x mod P) belongs to the motif M. The same period P and motif M apply to both halves; the axis row in the other half is at the same RELATIVE position within its half.
test2_t19Split-by-dots line and side-wise box growth/fill. Rule: - Exactly two gray dots (color 5) lie on two different grid edges. - The (invisible) line through these dots splits the grid into two halves. - Each half is processed independently: * If the half contains multiple shapes (8-connected components), fill the bounding box of all shapes in that half with gray. * If the half contains exactly one shape, grow it to an x*x square in its own color, where x = max(bbox_height, bbox_width) of that shape. - Nothing painted by the transformation may lie on the divider line.
test2_t2Cyclic color shift by leftmost-object order. Rule: - detect 8-connected non-zero objects - sort objects by the position of their leftmost cell (col, then row) - shift object colors to the right with wrap-around
test2_t20Space-invader style ship destruction. Rule: - Alien ships are foreground components (non-zero, non-gray, non-pink). - Pink cells (6) are shots fired vertically upward. - Shots are processed by shooter row from top to bottom. - Within the same row, shot order is chosen to maximize total ships destroyed eventually (looking ahead to lower rows). Deterministic tie-break: smallest lexicographic column-order permutation. - One shot destroys one whole ship (its entire connected component). - Gray cannon (5) at the bottom remains unchanged.
test2_t21Extract corner blocks and swap diagonally. Rule: - Determine corner size k from input as the bbox side length of the largest corner-assigned 8-connected non-background component. - Extract the four kxk corner patches from input. - Rearrange diagonally: TL <- BR, TR <- BL, BL <- TR, BR <- TL - Output is a 2k x 2k grid assembled from those four patches. Diagonal connectivity is used for component detection.
test2_t22Center anchor connects to same-color cells. Rule: - For each non-background color, choose its most central cell (nearest to grid center by squared Euclidean distance) as anchor. - Process colors in order of anchor row, top to bottom. - For each target cell of that color, draw an L path from anchor: vertical segment first (same column as anchor), then horizontal segment. - Current color overwrites existing cells, except cells that belong to colors that will be processed later (those are protected until their turn).
test2_t23Connect same-color shapes with gray straight lines. Interpretation used here: - Shapes are 8-connected components of equal color (diagonal touch connects). - For each color, every pair of its shapes connects at most once. - A connection is valid only when the endpoint neighborhood orientation matches on both ends of the segment (e.g. horizontal: left-neighbor at start and right-neighbor at end). - Among valid connections, use the shortest orthogonal straight segment. - Draw connections in gray (5), excluding component endpoint cells. - Remove all original colored shapes in the output.
test2_t24Virus jump-spread recoloring. Rule: - Exactly one red virus cell (color 2) is present; no other red cells exist. - Other cells form colored shapes (4-connected components of same color). - Virus spreads from infected cells to shapes by either: * direct orthogonal adjacency, * orthogonal jump over exactly one black cell, or * direct diagonal adjacency (distance (1,1)). - When a shape is reached, the whole shape becomes infected and can further spread from any of its cells. - Output recolors all infected shapes to red and removes the singular initial virus cell.
test2_t25Keep prototype-composable shapes, delete others. Interpretation: - Grid contains one foreground color split into multiple 4-connected shapes. - Prototype candidates are present shapes that: (a) cannot be composed from multiple non-overlapping copies of any other present shape (under symmetry), and (b) can compose at least one other present shape. - Among those candidates, the unique largest one is the prototype. - Any shape that can be exactly composed by one or more non-overlapping copies of the prototype (using translation/rotation/reflection) is kept. - All other shapes are removed.
test2_t26Fill symmetry-supported gaps in a colored outline. Rule: - The input shows a colored hollow outline plus one or two central cyan lines. - Each cyan line is a line of symmetry (vertical, horizontal, main diagonal, or anti-diagonal). - In the output, any gap position is filled if some other position in its symmetry orbit already contains a non-cyan colored cell. The copied color is the orbit's color. - If every non-line position in an orbit is empty, the orbit remains empty; the task does not complete the shape from global gestalt alone.
test2_t27The input contains several colored anchor shapes. The shapes can be partitioned into one or two square arrangements. Within each arrangement, the member shapes tile a filled 3x3, 4x4, or 5x5 square when rotations are allowed (up to five pieces on a 5x5). For each member shape, the output completes that square around the anchor while keeping that anchor fixed in place and orientation.
test2_t28Breakout-style diagonal bouncing stop. Task rule: - The input contains several obstacle cells/shapes, a single colored moving cell near the bottom, and a 2-cell grey diagonal pointer extending from that cell. - The pointer indicates the moving cell's initial diagonal direction. - Imagine the cell moving one step at a time in that direction, bouncing elastically off the grid walls (45-degree billiard motion). - Movement stops when the *next* step after any required wall-bounce would land on an already colored obstacle cell. The output shows the moving cell at its last empty position, with the original moving cell and grey pointer removed.
test2_t29Color-directed diagonal motion of V/L-shaped objects. Rule: - Each connected monochrome object moves rigidly in a direction determined only by its color: 6 (magenta): up-right (-1, +1) 3 (green): up-left (-1, -1) 4 (yellow): down-left (+1, -1) 8 (cyan): down-right (+1, +1) - Each object moves as far as possible until moving farther would place some cell outside the grid. Equivalently, it is translated by the maximal nonnegative k such that all translated cells remain inside the grid. - The objects are not rotated or deformed.
test2_t30The input shows vertical frame sequences with blank missing frames. The output fills those frames by continuing a simple visual progression.
test2_t31Mirror 2x2 blocks across a green divider unless blocked. Rule: - The grid is split into equal left/right halves by a full-height green vertical line. - Each non-green object is a solid 2x2 block. - Each block has a corresponding 2x2 target position on the opposite half at the same row and same within-half column. - A block moves to its target unless that target would cause a collision. - Collision is determined from target cells: if a target overlaps any original block cell, or if multiple blocks have overlapping targets, the involved block stays in its original position. All other blocks move. - The divider is preserved.
test2_t32Bidirectional color mapping across a horizontal separator. Rule: - The grid is divided into a top and bottom section by a full grey row. - Above the grey row, each row encodes a color correspondence: the uniform color on the left half maps to the uniform color on the right half. The mapping is two-way. - Below the grey row are colored blocks placed entirely on either the left half or the right half. - In the output, every bottom block is preserved in place and also recreated on the opposite half using the mapped color while keeping the same shape and row positions.
test2_t34Recover an occluded pattern, then rotate by the red-corner cue. Task rule: - The input contains a cyan square occluder with exactly one red cell at one corner. - The output is the pattern hidden beneath the square, rotated so that the red corner would move to the top-left of the square. * red already top-left -> no rotation * red top-right -> rotate hidden patch 90° counterclockwise * red bottom-left -> rotate hidden patch 90° clockwise * red bottom-right -> rotate hidden patch 180° Solver model classes supported: 1) nested concentric rectangular rings, 2) row-constant stripe fields recoverable by bridging across the square, and 3) diagonal-repeat fields with step (s, s) for small s.
test2_t35Repair the order of frame sequence. Rule: - The input shows a horizontal sequence of same-sized "image" frames separated by vertical cyan lines. - In the correct order, the frames show a natural visual progression. - The output reorders the frames into that progression.
test2_t36Follow the GPS-like relative-turn instructions. Rule: - The top row contains a yellow start token, then a sequence of (location color, turn color) pairs, then a cyan end token. - The lower part of the grid is a lattice of gray 3x3 node-tiles separated by blank spacer rows/cols. Each node has a colored center. - Start at the unique yellow node center (4). Move to a neighboring node whose color matches the first location token. There is no direction token for the start node. - At each visited internal node, use the paired direction token to turn relative to the direction you were just traveling: * blue (1) = straight, * red (2) = right, * green (3) = left. - Move one edge in that new direction to the next node. The next node must match the next location token, and after the final internal node, the final turn must take you to the cyan end node (8). - In the output, the top-row instructions are deleted and the taken route is drawn in yellow as a 1-cell-thick polyline through the visited nodes and corridors. The cyan destination center remains cyan.
test2_t37Row-wise shift with top-row recoloring markers. Rule: - The input contains a large single-color shape away from the top row and left column. - Cells in the left column mark row shifts: * red (2) means shift that row's shape cells right by 1, * green (3) means shift that row's shape cells left by 1. - Cells in the top row mark highlight colors for columns. - For a row that is shifted, every shape cell in that row moves one step in the row's marked direction. Before moving, if the cell's column has a nonzero top-row marker, its color is changed to that top-row marker color. - Shape cells on unmarked rows do not move and are not recolored. - All markers in the top row and left column are removed in the output.
test2_t38Recover the atomic template from an inverse fractal. Rule: - The input is a self-similar fractal built from a small atomic template. - The atomic template is a binary shape of size HxW. - Starting from a single filled cell, each recursion replaces every filled cell by a copy of the whole template, so after depth n the grid size is H^n x W^n. - Most occupied cells in the final fractal use a base color; a small number of occupied cells use a distinct highlight color. - The output is the atomic template itself, using the base color on occupied template cells, except that a template cell is recolored with the highlight color iff at least one highlighted input cell belongs to that template position's residue class (equivalently: has those coordinates modulo H and W).
test2_t39Shortest directed path across cannon-islands. Rule: - The input contains several 3x3 hollow square "islands" laid out on a grid. - The island borders use one outline color. - A red cell (color 2) at the midpoint of one side of an island is a one-way cannon, allowing movement from that island to the adjacent island in that direction. - One island center contains a yellow cell (color 4), the source. - One island center contains a cyan cell (color 8), the target. - Follow the unique shortest directed path from source to target. - In the output, every red cannon used on that path is recolored gray (color 5), the source center is cleared to background, and the target center is recolored yellow.
test2_t40Remove red-marked hollow blocks and unsupported blocks. Rule: - The input contains several hollow rectangular blocks. - Some blocks are marked red by filling the entire interior of the block with red cells (color 2). - In the output, every red-marked block is removed. - Then any remaining block that is no longer supported is also removed, recursively. - A block is supported if either: * its bottom edge lies on the bottom row of the grid, or * there exists at least one remaining block whose top edge is exactly one row below its bottom edge, with overlapping horizontal span.
test2_t41Refraction through a dotted vertical surface. Rule: - The input contains a vertical dotted gray line (color 5) and a single colored cell to its left. - The colored cell is imagined to move horizontally right, passing through the surface and ending at the far-right edge on the same row. - From the intersection point with the vertical line, a second copy moves diagonally up-right until it hits either the top edge or the right edge. - The output contains the same dotted line and exactly those two resulting colored cells on the right; the original left-side colored cell disappears.
test2_t42Correct shifted color-scheme pattern copies across sections. Rule: - The grid is split into equal left/right halves. - It is also split into several horizontal sections. - In each section, the left half contains a two-color pattern. - The right half should contain the *same pattern* but recolored using the two colors from the section below (wrapping from bottom to top), except that a small number of cells are incorrect. - The output is the full grid with those right-half mistakes corrected.
test2_t43Reverse the front-to-back occlusion order of objects. Rule: - The input shows several recognizable objects stacked/occluding one another. - Their front-to-back order is uniquely identifiable from the visible overlaps. - Reconstruct each full object, then reverse the depth order: the previous backmost object becomes frontmost, and the previous frontmost object becomes backmost.
test2_t44Count rotations needed to align outline gaps. Rule: - The input has two cells of one marker color: one at the center of a nest of concentric square outlines, and one on the grid edge aligned horizontally or vertically with the center. - Each square outline has exactly one gap at the midpoint of one side. - Starting from the innermost outline and moving outward, count how many clockwise 90-degree rotations are needed to move that outline's gap to the side facing the edge marker / clear path. - The output has width 3 and one row per outline. A count n in {0,1,2,3} is encoded by filling the first n cells of the row with that outline's color.
test2_t45Toggle shadow and light from a yellow marker. Rule: - A yellow (4) marker is placed at a corner or the midpoint of one edge. - The grid has a single non-black/non-yellow background color. - Non-background, non-yellow cells cast black shadows away from the marker: * from an edge midpoint, shadows are horizontal/vertical; * from a corner, shadows are diagonal. - Yellow cells do not cast black shadows. - If a yellow cell lies in another object's shadow, it shines light in the same direction instead: consecutive background cells after it are restored to the background color until the ray hits another non-background object.
test2_t464-color sudoku over 2x2 quadrants. Rule: - The grid is always 5x5. - The middle row and middle column are gray (5), splitting the grid into four 2x2 quadrants. - The four non-gray colors must fill the sixteen non-gray cells. - No row, column, or 2x2 quadrant may contain the same color twice. - Inputs reveal exactly four colored cells, one of each color; the output is the unique valid completion.
test2_t47XOR two halves separated by a gray line. Rule: - The input consists of two equal-size halves separated by a full gray (5) line. - The halves share one background color. - Each half has exactly one non-background foreground color. - For each corresponding location, output: - the left/top foreground color if only the first half has foreground there; - the right/bottom foreground color if only the second half has foreground there; - 0 if neither or both halves have foreground there.
test2_t48Uniquely completable four-color map coloring. Rule: - The last row begins with four palette colors. - In the map area above, one boundary color (not in the palette) forms the map walls, enclosing several regions. - Some regions are already colored with palette colors; at least one region is left uncolored (0). - Complete the empty regions so that adjacent regions never share a color.
test2_t49Aligned 2x2 nodes connected by an MST. Rule: - The input contains several 2x2 nodes, all in one non-black/non-gray color. - Two nodes can be connected only when their 2x2 blocks have the same top row or the same left column. - The weight of a valid connection is the gap distance between the two blocks, measured in cells between their facing edges. - Draw a minimal spanning tree of the alignment graph using gray (5) cells. Corridors are two cells wide, matching the width/height of the 2x2 nodes.
test2_t50Functional-graph cycle detection → polygon drawing. Layout ------ Nodes occupy a regular (R_n × C_n) sub-grid inside the pixel grid. Each node sits at pixel position (r0 + ri * s, c0 + ci * s) where (ri, ci) is its 0-based node-grid index, r0/c0 are the top-left offsets, and s is the node spacing (s >= 3). Each node has exactly one outgoing direction (up/down/left/right) stored as a unit vector (dr, dc). The orange (7) indicator is placed one pixel from the node in that direction. The next node is s pixels away in the same direction.
